Minimal generating sets of large powers of bivariate monomial ideals Thanks: This research was funded in part by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF) [10.55776/DOC78]. For open access purposes, the authors have applied a CC BY public copyright license to any author-accepted manuscript version arising from this submission.
Jutta Rath, Roswitha Rissner

Abstract
. It is known that for a monomial ideal I I , the number of minimal generators, μ ⁡ ( I n ) \mu(I^{n}) , eventually follows a polynomial pattern for increasing n n . In general, little is known about the power at which this pattern emerges. Even less is known about the exact form of the minimal generators after this power. Let s ≥ μ ⁡ ( I ) ​ ( d 2 − 1 ) + 1 s\geq\mu(I)(d^{2}-1)+1 , where d d is a constant bounded above by the maximal x x - or y y -degree appearing in the set 𝖦 ⁡ ( I ) \mathsf{G}(I) of minimal generators of I I . We show that every higher power I s + ℓ I^{s+\ell} for any ℓ ≥ 0 \ell\geq 0 can be constructed from certain subideals of I s I^{s} . This provides an explicit description of 𝖦 ⁡ ( I s + ℓ ) \mathsf{G}(I^{s+\ell}) in terms of 𝖦 ⁡ ( I s ) \mathsf{G}(I^{s}) . Given 𝖦 ⁡ ( I s ) \mathsf{G}(I^{s}) , this construction significantly reduces computational complexity in determining larger powers of I I . This further enables us to explicitly compute μ ⁡ ( I n ) \mu(I^{n}) for all n ≥ s n\geq s in terms of a linear polynomial in n n . We include runtime measurements for the attached implementation in SageMath.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
13C99, 13E15, 68W30, 13F20, 13A15, 13B22

1.
Introduction

Powers of monomial ideals have been studied in many different
contexts. Brodmann
[
2
,
3
]
showed that the set
\Ass(R/I^{n})
of
associated primes stabilizes for large
n
and that

\operatorname{depth}(R/I^{n})
eventually becomes constant. Since
then, much research has extended these results. For a recent survey,
we refer to Carlini, Hà, Harbourne, and Van Tuyl’s lecture
notes
[
4
]
.

When studying powers of a monomial ideal, its minimal generators play
a crucial role. However, it is far from trivial to determine which of
the
n
-fold products of (minimal) generators of
I
are minimal
generators of
I^{n}
and very little is known for monomial ideals, even
in the bivariate case.

The existing research mostly focuses on the number
\mu(I^{n})
of
minimal generators of
I^{n}
rather than the actual set of minimal
generators. Indeed, the emphasis lies on small powers, as
\mu(I^{n})

is eventually described by a polynomial—the Hilbert polynomial of
the fiber ring of
I
. Eliahou, Herzog, and
Saem
[
5
]
studied the question
how small
\mu(I^{2})
can be in terms of
\mu(I)
for a bivariate
monomial ideal
I
. For any given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, Abdolmaleki and
Kumashiro
[
1
]
construct a
bivariate monomial ideal
I
such that

\mu(I)>\mu(I^{2})>\cdots>\mu(I^{n})
. Gasanova
[
6
]

showed that for every
d
there exists a monomial ideal
I
in any
number of variables such that the inequality
\mu(I)>\mu(I^{n})
holds
for any
n\leq d
.

While the unexpected behavior of small powers is fascinating, our
focus is set on large
n
. The asymptotic behavior of the Hilbert
function gives reason to suspect that eventually the actual set of
minimal generators of
I^{n}
behaves well, in the sense that
cancellations among the
n
-fold products of generators of
I
can be
predicted.

The main objective of this paper is to describe the sets of minimal
generators of large powers of monomial ideals
I
in

\mathsf{k}[x,y]
, where
\mathsf{k}
is a field.

(a)

I=({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{4}},xy,y^{3})

(b)

J=(x^{2},{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y^{2}})

(c)

I\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.82289pt}{-1.125pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.91 -1.56)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}J={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}y^{2}}I+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}x^{4}}J

Fig. 1
.
Visualization of the ideal link

Specifically, we show that there exists
s_{0}
such that for all

n\geq s\geq s_{0}
every segment of the staircase diagram—and
consequently, the set of minimal generators—of
I^{n}
is already
determined by the staircase diagram of
I^{s}
. In other words, for

n\geq s
, the staircase diagram of
I^{n}
can be built by aligning the
staircase diagrams of certain subideals of
I^{s}
. We prove that

s_{0}\leq\mu(I)(d^{2}-1)+1
, where
d
is a constant depending on the
degrees of the minimal generators of
I
and is at most the maximal

x
- or
y
-degree appearing in
\mathsf{G}(I)
.

To formalize the process of aligning staircases of ideals, we
introduce the concept of the
link of ideals
with respect to

y
, denoted by
\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.13657pt}{-1.25pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.34 -1.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}
(Definition
4.7
). The
staircase of the ideal
I\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.13657pt}{-1.25pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.34 -1.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}J
is obtained by shifting
I
in

y
-direction and
J
in
x
-direction such that their staircases meet
in precisely one minimal generator. This is illustrated in
Figure
1
.

In Theorem
5.4
we show that
for all
s
larger than an explicitly given power
s_{0}
, all powers

I^{s+\ell}
for any
\ell\geq 0
can be written as the link of specific
subideals of
I^{s}
.

It immediately follows that

\mu(I^{s+\ell})=\mu(I^{s})+\ell\cdot\left(\mu(I^{s+1})-\mu(I^{s})\right)

for
s\geq s_{0}
and
\ell\geq 0
, see Corollary
5.11
.

Moreover, the minimal generators of
I\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 9.53 0 C 9.53 5.26 5.26 9.53 0 9.53 C -5.26 9.53 -9.53 5.26 -9.53 0 C -9.53 -5.26 -5.26 -9.53 0 -9.53 C 5.26 -9.53 9.53 -5.26 9.53 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.13657pt}{-1.25pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.34 -1.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}J
can be determined by

\mu(I)+\mu(J)-1
many additions of (monomial) exponents. Thus,

\mathsf{G}\!\left(I^{s+\ell}\right)
can be computed from
\mathsf{G}\!\left(I^{s}\right)
in

\mathcal{O}(\ell)
additions of non-negative integers
(Corollary
5.9
).

This paper is structured as follows:
Section
2
summarizes the necessary
background about the integral closure of bivariate monomial ideals.
In Section
3
, we establish that the
lattice points on the boundary of the Newton polyhedron of
I
play a
special role among the minimal generators of powers of
I
. We call
them (weakly) persistent generators
(Definitions
3.1

and
3.3
), as their powers remain minimal
generators of all powers of
I
. The main result of this section is
Theorem
3.16
. This theorem is a refinement of the
known fact that the ideal generated by the persistent generators is a
reduction of

I

[
10
, Proposition 2.1]
. It allows
us to break down large enough powers of
I
into sums of simpler
components, using its weakly persistent generators. In
Section
4
we describe the minimal
generators of each such component separately
(Theorem
4.4
) and then determine
their sum in Theorem
4.16
.
Section
5
combines the results of
Section
3
and
Section
4
to the main results of this
paper (Theorem
5.4
and
Corollary
5.11
). We provide an implementation to
compute
I^{s+\ell}
in SageMath
1
1

1

The program code associated
with this paper is available as ancillary file from the arXiv page

https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21466
of this paper.
. We conclude
the paper with examples and runtime measurements in practice in
Section
6
.

We suspect that powers of ideals in more than two variables eventually
show analogous patterns. The immediate challenge is the generalization
of the link operation, as it strongly depends on the bivariate
structure.

We point out that the presented approach is not suitable to determine
minimal generators of small ideal powers.

2.
Preliminaries

a
b

g

Fig. 2
.
Left: The integral closure of
(x^{a},y^{b})
adds
all lattice points above the line connecting
(a,0)
and

(0,b)
(red). Right: Visualization of Fact
2.4
(2).

Throughout this paper we frequently identify a monomial
x^{a}y^{b}
with
the lattice point
(a,b)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Note that in the bivariate
case, the minimal generators of an ideal are always of the form

x^{a_{i}}y^{b_{i}}
with

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle<a_{1}<\dots<a_{n},\text{ and }

\displaystyle b_{0}

\displaystyle>b_{1}>\dots>b_{n}.

We always assume that
I
is not a principal ideal. Moreover, many
properties of monomial ideals, such as the number of minimal
generators, remain invariant under shifts. Therefore, when it
simplifies notation, we divide
I
by the greatest common divisor of
all its monomials, denoted by
\gcd(I)
.

.

.

Definition 2.2
.

For a monomial ideal
I
, the
integral closure

\overline{I}
is
defined as the set of all monomials
f
for which there exists

n\in\mathbb{N}
with
f^{n}\in I^{n}
.

Notation 2.3
.

We denote by
(E)
the ideal generated by the set

E\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
.

Fact 2.4

(cf.
[
9
, Proposition 1.4.6]
)
.

For
a
,
b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and a monomial
g\in\mathsf{k}[x,y]
the following are
equivalent:

(1)

x^{u}y^{v}\in\overline{(x^{a},y^{b})}

(2)

g\cdot x^{u}y^{v}\in\overline{(g\cdot x^{a},g\cdot y^{b})}

(3)

\frac{u}{a}+\frac{v}{b}\geq 1

Moreover,
\frac{u}{a}+\frac{v}{b}=1
implies

x^{u}y^{v}\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\overline{(x^{a},y^{b})}\right)
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
f
,
g
,
h\in\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be monomials such that

f\notin(g,h)
. We say that
f

lies between

g

and
h
if

\displaystyle\min\{\deg_{x}g,\deg_{x}h\}

\displaystyle<\deg_{x}f\text{ and}

\displaystyle\min\{\deg_{y}g,\deg_{y}h\}

\displaystyle<\deg_{y}f.

Remark 2.6
.

Let
f
be a monomial in between two other monomials,
g
and
h
,
in
\mathsf{k}[x,y]
. Geometrically, Fact
2.4
says that
f
is in
the integral closure of
(g,h)
if and only if it lies above the
line passing through
g
and
h
, see
Figure
2
.

Throughout, we use
\bullet
as a placeholder for either
x
or

y
, with the choice remaining fixed within a given context.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be a monomial ideal. We define the

\bullet
-distance

\dist_{\bullet}\!I
of
I
as

\displaystyle\dist_{\bullet}\!I

\displaystyle\coloneqq\max\{\deg_{\bullet}g\mid g\in\mathsf{G}({I}_{\mathbin{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to11.52pt{\vbox to13.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.75883pt\lower-4.86772pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}})\}.

For a set of monomials
E\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
, we define

\dist_{\bullet}\!E
as
\dist_{\bullet}
of the ideal
(E)
.

Remark 2.8
.

(1)

The
\bullet
-distance of an anchored ideal
I
is the distance
from the origin to the Newton polyhedron of
I
measured along the

\bullet
-axis.

(2)

Let
f
,
g
, and
h
be monomials such that
f
lies between

g
and
h
. Then

\dist_{\bullet}(h,f)+\dist_{\bullet}(f,g)=\dist_{\bullet}(h,g)
.

(3)

\dist_{\bullet}\!I=\max\{\deg_{\bullet}g\mid g\in\mathsf{G}(I)\}-\min\{\deg_{\bullet}g\mid g\in\mathsf{G}(I)\}
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
g
and
h
be two monomials. We define the non-standard grading

\deg_{g,h}
additively by setting

\displaystyle\deg_{g,h}(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\dist_{y}(g,h)\text{ and}

\displaystyle\deg_{g,h}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\dist_{x}(g,h).

We set
\mathsf{d}_{g,h}\coloneqq\deg_{g,h}(g)=\deg_{g,h}(h)
.

Remark 2.10
.

(1)

\mathsf{d}_{x^{a},y^{b}}=ab

(2)

Let
g
and
h
be two monomials and

x^{\alpha}y^{\beta}\coloneqq\gcd(g,h)
. Then

\deg_{g,h}(x^{u}y^{v})\geq\mathsf{d}_{g,h}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\frac{u-\alpha}{\dist_{x}(g,h)}+\frac{v-\beta}{\dist_{y}(g,h)}\geq 1,

where equality holds simultaneously on both sides. Note that here,

x^{u}y^{v}
does not necessarily lie between
g
and
h
.

(3)

Geometrically, the equivalence in (2) states

\deg_{g,h}(x^{u}y^{v})\geq\mathsf{d}_{g,h}
if and only if
x^{u}y^{v}
lies
above the line through
g
and
h
. Equality holds if it is
exactly on the line.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
f
,
g
,
h\in\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be monomials such that
f
lies between

g
and
h
.

Then the following assertions are equivalent:

(1)

f\in\overline{(g,h)}

(2)

\deg_{g,h}(f)\geq\mathsf{d}_{g,h}

(3)

\deg_{f,h}(g)\leq\mathsf{d}_{f,h}

(4)

\deg_{g,f}(h)\leq\mathsf{d}_{g,f}

Moreover,
\deg_{g,h}(f)=\mathsf{d}_{g,h}
implies
f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(\overline{(g,h)}\right)
.

Proof.

(1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2) follows from
Remark
2.10
(2) and Fact
2.4
.
(2)
\Leftrightarrow
(3) and (2)
\Leftrightarrow
(4) follow from
Remark
2.10
(3).
∎

3.
The role of persistent generators

To analyze higher powers of monomial ideals we introduce the notion of

(weakly) persistent generators
. They are monomials whose
powers continue to appear among the minimal generators of
I^{n}
for
every
n
. We use these generators to decompose large powers of
I

into simpler components.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
I
be a monomial ideal in
\mathsf{k}[x,y]
. We say
f\in\mathsf{G}(I)

is a
persistent generator of
I
if

f\notin\overline{(g,h)}
for all monomials
g
,

h\in I\setminus\{f\}
. We denote the set of all persistent
generators of
I
by
P(I)
.

Remark 3.2
.

(1)

The minimal generators of
I
with maximal
x
-degree and

y
-degree, respectively, are persistent.

(2)

The persistent generators of
I
are the corners of the Newton polyhedron of
I
.

(3)

If
f\in P(I)
, then
f^{n}\in\mathsf{G}\!\left(I^{n}\right)
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
I
be a monomial ideal in
\mathsf{k}[x,y]
. We say that

f\in\mathsf{G}(I)
is
weakly persistent
, if

f^{n}\in\mathsf{G}(I^{n})
holds for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by

P^{\ast}(I)
the set of all weakly persistent generators of
I
.

Remark 3.4
.

By Remark
3.2
(3), the inclusion

P(I)\subseteq P^{\ast}(I)
holds. Let
g_{1}
, …,
g_{k+1}
be
the persistent generators of
I
, ordered in descending
y
-degree.
With Proposition
3.5
below we will
see that

P^{\ast}(I)=P(I)\cup\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}(I)\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{g_{i},g_{i+1}}(f)=\mathsf{d}_{g_{i},g_{i+1}}\text{ for some }1\leq i\leq k\right\}\!.

Hence, the weakly persistent generators correspond to the lattice
points on the boundary of the Newton polyhedron of
I
, see
Remark
2.10
(3).

By definition,
f\in\overline{(g,h)}
is equivalent to
f^{n}\in(g,h)^{n}
for
some
n
. In the next proposition, we explicitly determine such an

n
. In addition, we show that if
f\notin\overline{(g,h)}
, a similar
relation holds among the three polynomials.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
g
,
h
,
f\in\mathsf{k}[x,y]
with
f
lying between
g
and
h
, and
define

\alpha\coloneqq\dist_{\bullet}(f,h)\quad\text{and}\quad n\coloneqq\dist_{\bullet}(g,h).

Then the following assertions hold:

(1)

If
f\in\overline{(g,h)}
then
g^{\alpha}h^{n-\alpha}\mid f^{n}

and hence
f^{n}\in(g,h)^{n}
.

(2)

If
f\notin\overline{(g,h)}
then
f^{n}\mid g^{\alpha}h^{n-\alpha}

and hence
g^{\alpha}h^{n-\alpha}\in(f)^{n}
.

Moreover,
\deg_{g,h}(f)=\mathsf{d}_{g,h}
if and only if there exist

n\in\mathbb{N}
and
0\leq k\leq n
such that
g^{k}h^{n-k}=f^{n}
.

Proof.

Recall that
n-\alpha=\dist_{\bullet}(g,f)

(Remark
2.8
(1)). Since
f
lies between
g
and
h

it follows that all three monomials are divisible by

\gcd(g,h)
. Therefore, we can assume that
g=y^{b}
,
h=x^{a}
, and

f=x^{u}y^{v}
with
0<u<a
and
0<v<b
.
It suffices to show the assertions for
\bullet=x
.
It follows that
n=a
and
\alpha=a-u
. Note that

\deg_{x}\!\big(x^{au}y^{b(a-u)}\big)=au=\deg_{x}\!\left(f^{a}\right)\!.

By Fact
2.4
,
f\in\overline{(g,h)}
if and only if

\frac{u}{a}+\frac{v}{b}\geq 1
which, in turn, is equivalent to

\deg_{y}\!\big(x^{au}y^{b(a-u)}\big)=b(a-u)\leq av=\deg_{y}\!\left(f^{a}\right)\!.

For the last assertion note that the right-hand side holds if and
only if

ak=un\text{ and }b(n-k)=vn,

which is equivalent to
\frac{u}{a}+\frac{v}{b}=1
. The latter
is equivalent to the left-hand side,
cf. Remark
2.10
(2).
∎

Remark 3.6
.

Note that in the second assertion of
Proposition
3.5
, the assumption
that
f\notin\overline{(g,h)}
implies that the equality

\deg_{g,h}(f)=\mathsf{d}_{g,h}
cannot hold. In particular, we have
that
f^{n}\neq g^{\alpha}h^{n-\alpha}
.

Remark 3.7
.

The bound
\min\{\dist_{x}(g,h),\dist_{y}(g,h)\}
for
n

in Proposition
3.5
is sharp, see
Example
3.8
below.

Example 3.8
.

Let
g=y^{5}
and
h=x^{6}
. Then

\min\{\dist_{x}(g,h),\dist_{y}(g,h)\}=5
.

(1)

The monomial
f=x^{5}y
is an element of
\overline{(g,h)}
for which
one can easily verify that
f^{n}\notin(x^{6},y^{5})^{n}
for
n\leq 4
.

(2)

The monomial
f=xy^{4}
lies between
g
and
h
such that

f\notin\overline{(g,h)}
. A straightforward computation shows that
there is no
n<5
such that there exist
\alpha
,
\beta
with

\alpha+\beta=n
and
g^{\alpha}h^{\beta}\in(f)^{n}
.

Notation 3.9
.

Let
F=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{k+1}\}
be a set of monomials such that

g_{1}
, …,
g_{k+1}
are ordered in descending
y
-degree. We
set

\delta_{F}\coloneqq\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\!\big\{\min\{\dist_{x}(g_{i},g_{i+1}),\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})\}\big\}-1.

Remark 3.10
.

(1)

If
g
lies between
g_{1}
and
g_{k+1}
, then

\delta_{F}\geq\delta_{F\cup\{g\}}
.

(2)

If
f\in\mathsf{G}(I)
is not weakly persistent, then
Proposition
3.5
implies that

f^{\delta_{P(I)}}\notin\mathsf{G}(I^{\delta_{P(I)}})
.

By definition, powers of weakly persistent generators remain minimal
generators. Proposition
3.5
yields a
description of powers of all elements of
I
:

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
I
be a monomial ideal in
\mathsf{k}[x,y]
and let

P(I)\subseteq P\subseteq P^{\ast}(I)
such that

P=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{k+1}\}
and
g_{1},
…,
g_{k+1}
are ordered
in descending
y
-degree. For every
n\geq\delta_{P}
and
f\in I

there exist
1\leq i\leq k
and
a\leq\delta_{P}
such that

f^{n}\in(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{n-a}\cdot f^{a}.

Proof.

For elements in
P
, the assertion trivially holds. Note that the
elements
f\in I\setminus P
are not persistent. By definition,
there exist
g
,
h\in I\setminus\{f\}
with
f\in\overline{(g,h)}
. We
can choose
g
and
h
to be in
P
such that no other element of

P
lies between them, meaning
g=g_{i}
and
h=g_{i+1}
for some

1\leq i\leq k
. We write

d\coloneqq\min\{\dist_{x}(g_{i},g_{i+1}),\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})\}
. Then
n\geq\delta_{P}\geq d-1
and we can write
n=qd+a

with
q\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
a\leq d-1
. It follows from
Proposition
3.5
(1) that

f^{n}=f^{qd+a}\in(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{qd}\cdot f^{a}.

∎

Corollary
3.11
immediately yields:

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be a monomial ideal,
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and let

P(I)\subseteq P\subseteq P^{\ast}(I)
. Then every minimal
generator of
I^{n}
is of the form

(3.1)

\prod_{g\in P}g^{\ell_{g}}\cdot\prod_{f\in\mathsf{G}(I)\setminus P}f^{k_{f}},

where
0\leq k_{f}\leq\delta_{P}
for all
f\in\mathsf{G}(I)\setminus P

and
\sum_{g}\ell_{g}+\sum_{f}k_{f}=n
.

Example 3.13
.

Let
I=(g,f,h)
with
g=y^{5}
,
f=x^{5}y
, and
h=x^{6}
from
Example
3.8
(1) with
P=P(I)=\{g,h\}
. Since

f^{5}=xgh^{4}
, it follows that, for instance, the monomial

g^{20}h^{3}f^{12}=g^{20}h^{3}f^{2\cdot 5+2}\in I^{35}
is divisible
by
g^{20+2}\cdot h^{3+8}\cdot f^{2}\in I^{35}
.

Remark 3.14
.

Singla
[
10
, Proposition 2.1]

established that the ideal
\mathfrak{a}
, generated by the
persistent generators
P(I)
, is a reduction of
I
. That is, there
exists
\delta\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

I^{\delta+n}=\mathfrak{a}^{n}I^{\delta}.

Singla’s result applies to monomial ideals in any number of
variables. Through Corollary
3.12
we
recover Singla’s result for the bivariate case. We further show
that
\delta
can be chosen to be at most

|\mathsf{G}(I)\setminus P(I)|\cdot\delta_{P(I)}
. The minimal choice
for
\delta
is called
reduction number
of
I
with respect
to
\mathfrak{a}
. This number measures from which power on the
ideal
\mathfrak{a}
determines the behavior of all further powers
of
I
.

With Proposition
3.5
(2), we further
refine the statement of Corollary
3.12
in
Theorem
3.16
below.

Notation 3.15
.

For a monomial ideal
I
and
P(I)\subseteq P\subseteq P^{\ast}(I)
. We set

d_{P}\coloneqq\begin{cases}\min\{\dist_{x}\!I,\dist_{y}\!I\}-2,&\text{if }|P|>2,\\
0,&\text{if }|P|=2.\end{cases}

Note that
|P|>2
implies that

\min\{\dist_{x}\!I,\dist_{y}\!I\}\geq 2
.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
I
be a monomial ideal in
\mathsf{k}[x,y]
and let

P(I)\subseteq P\subseteq P^{\ast}(I)
such that

P=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{k+1}\}
and
g_{1},
…,
g_{k+1}
are ordered
in descending
y
-degree. Further, let

D\geq(\mu(I)-|P|)\cdot\delta_{P}+|P|\cdot d_{P}
. Then for all

\ell\geq 0
,

I^{D+\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{\ell}I^{D}.

Proof.

The inclusion “
\supseteq
” is trivial. We prove
“
\subseteq
”. Write
N\coloneqq\mathsf{G}(I)\setminus P
. By
Corollary
3.12
we can write

I^{D+\ell}=I^{\delta+n}=\mathfrak{a}^{n}I^{\delta}
where

\delta\coloneqq|N|\cdot\delta_{P}
, and

n\coloneqq D-\delta+\ell\geq(k+1)d_{P}+\ell
, and
\mathfrak{a}
is
the ideal generated by
P
. Thus, every minimal generator
F
of

I^{\delta+n}
is of the form
F=g\cdot f
, where
g
is a product
of
n
elements in
P
and
f\in I^{\delta}
.

Claim.
There exists
1\leq i\leq k
such that

g\in(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{n-(k+1)d_{P}}\mathfrak{a}^{(k+1)d_{P}}.

We write
g=g_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots g_{k+1}^{n_{k}}
, where
n_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

with
\sum_{i}n_{i}=n
. The assertion of the claim holds trivially in
the following cases:

(1)

at most one
n_{i}>d_{P}
,

(2)

n_{j},n_{j+1}>d_{P}
for some
1\leq j\leq k
and
n_{i}\leq d_{P}
for
all
i\notin\{j,j+1\}
.

Otherwise, we take
a\coloneqq\min\{i\mid n_{i}>d_{P}\}
and

b\coloneqq\max\{i\mid n_{i}>d_{P}\}
. Note that
a+1<b
. The following
argument may be repeated as needed; in every step either
a

increases strictly or
b
decreases strictly. For readability, we
may therefore assume without loss of generality that
a=1
and

b=k+1
. By Proposition
3.5
(2),
we then have

g_{2}^{\dist_{\bullet}(g_{1},g_{k+1})}\mid g_{1}^{\dist_{\bullet}(g_{2},g_{k+1})}g_{k+1}^{\dist_{\bullet}(g_{1},g_{2})}.

Since
g
is a minimal generator, equality must hold, so

g=g_{1}^{n_{1}-\dist_{\bullet}(g_{2},g_{k+1})}g_{2}^{n_{2}+\dist_{\bullet}(g_{1},g_{k+1})}g_{3}^{n_{3}}\cdots g_{k}^{n_{k}}g_{k+1}^{n_{k+1}-\dist_{\bullet}(g_{1},g_{2})}.

We iteratively apply
Proposition
3.5
(2) until at least
one of the exponents of
g_{1}
or
g_{2}
becomes
\leq d_{P}
. At this
stage, we redefine
a^{\prime}\coloneqq\min\{i\mid n_{i}^{\prime}>d_{P}\}
and

b^{\prime}\coloneqq\max\{i\mid n_{i}^{\prime}>d_{P}\}
where
n_{i}^{\prime}
is the new exponent
of
g_{i}
. Now
a^{\prime}>a
or
b^{\prime}<b
must hold. Hence, by repeating this
argument from the top we eventually must reach one of the trivial
cases (1) or (2), where the claim follows immediately.
∎

Example 3.17
.

Let
I=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
with
g_{1}=y^{5}
,
g_{2}=xy^{4}
, and
g_{3}=x^{6}

from Example
3.8
. Then

P(I)=P^{\ast}(I)=\{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}\}
. Since
g_{2}^{5}\mid g_{1}^{4}g_{3}
,
it follows that, for instance, the monomial

g_{1}^{11}g_{2}^{102}g_{3}^{42}
is an element of

(g_{1}^{3}\cdot g_{2}^{102+10}\cdot g_{3}^{40})\subseteq(g_{2},g_{3})^{152}I^{3}
.

Remark 3.18
.

In Example
3.17
, a direct computation shows that

D=3
is large enough, although Theorem
3.16
gives a
lower bound of
9
. In general, a tight bound for the minimal
value for
D
depends on all the exponents in
\mathsf{G}(I)
.

Remark 3.19
.

The set
P
may be chosen closer to either
P(I)
or
P^{\ast}(I)
,
depending on the specific context in which
Theorem
3.16
is applied. For instance, if the
objective is to minimize
(\mu(I)-|P|)\cdot\delta_{P}+|P|\cdot d_{P}
,
then
P
can be selected based on the values of
d_{P}

and
\delta_{P}
.

4.
Ideals with regular staircase factors

Fig. 3
.

In this section we study the minimal generators of sums of ideals of
the form
(g,h)^{n}J
, in light of Theorem
3.16
. Here,

g
and
h
are monomials, and
J
is a (fixed) anchored monomial
ideal in
\mathsf{k}[x,y]
. The main result of this section is
Theorem
4.16
.

We begin with the special case
(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J
for
u
,
v\in\mathbb{N}
. By
drawing its exponents in the
xy
-plane, the ideal
(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}
looks
like a “regular staircase” in the sense that all
n
steps in its
staircase are of the same size. The minimal generators of the product

(x^{u},y^{u})^{n}J
are of the form
x^{u(n-i)}y^{vi}f
with

f\in\mathsf{G}(J)
. In general, not all elements of this form are
minimal generators, as divisibility relations may occur among them.
In Figure
3
we visualize the potential
cancellations among the minimal generators in the product

(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J
. Although Figure
3
does
not show the actual generators of the ideal
J
, it illustrates how
the overlaps of the shifted copies of
J
form a
repeating pattern as
n
increases. We formalize this “pattern
repetition” in Theorem
4.4
below.
Before that, we establish in Lemma
4.2
that
partitioning the elements of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J
based on their
y
-degrees
reveals divisibilities by certain powers of
x^{u}
or

y^{v}
. Figure
4
visualizes this
partition.

Remark 4.1
.

The choice of partitioning by
y
-degree is arbitrary; all results
of this section remain valid if we instead partition by
x
-degree,
simply by interchanging the roles of
x
and
y
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
u
,
v\in\mathbb{N}
and
J\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be an anchored monomial
ideal. Moreover, let

r\geq\left\lceil\frac{\dist_{y}\!J}{v}\right\rceil
and
n\geq r
. For
r\leq j\leq n
, we set

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}F\geq jv\right\}\!\text{, and }

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{j}

\displaystyle=\left\{F\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}F<jv\right\}\!.

Then, for
r\leq j\leq n
,

\mathcal{U}_{j}\subseteq y^{v(j-r)}\cdot(x^{u},y^{v})^{n-(j-r)}J\text{ and }\mathcal{L}_{j}\subseteq x^{u(n-j)}\cdot(x^{u},y^{v})^{j}J.

In particular, for
r\leq j\leq n-1
,

\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{L}_{j+1}=x^{u(n-(j+1))}y^{v(j-r)}\cdot\left\{f\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\,\,\middle|\,\,rv\leq\deg_{y}f<(r+1)v\right\}\!.

j=3
j=4
j=5
j=6

Fig. 4
.
We partition
(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J
into sections based on the

y
-degree (indicated by dashed lines). Additionally, note that
the upper left corner of each rectangle is in
(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J

(since we assumed
J
to be anchored). This bounds the

x
-degree in each
y
-section; see
Remark
4.3
.

Proof.

For the first two inclusions we write
F=x^{u(n-i)}y^{vi}\tilde{f}

with
0\leq i\leq n
and
\tilde{f}\in\mathsf{G}(J)
and separate into
two cases.

\mathcal{U}_{j}
: Note that
\deg_{y}\tilde{f}\leq\dist_{y}\!J
. Hence,
\deg_{y}F\geq jv
implies

i\geq j-\frac{\dist_{y}\!J}{v}\geq j-r\geq 0
, that is,

F=y^{v(j-r)}\cdot f
with

f=x^{u(n-i)}y^{v(i-(j-r))}\tilde{f}\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{n-(j-r)}J
.

\mathcal{L}_{j}
: The condition
\deg_{y}F<jv
implies
i\leq j
and

n-i\geq n-j\geq 0
. Therefore,
F=x^{u(n-j)}\cdot f
with

f=x^{u(j-i)}y^{vi}\tilde{f}\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{j}J
.

Finally, for the last equality, “
\subseteq
” follows from the
above while “
\supseteq
” is obvious.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

As preparation for later arguments, we provide a bound on the

x
-degrees of elements in
\mathcal{U}_{j}
: Let
J
be an anchored
monomial ideal with
b\coloneqq\dist_{y}\!J
. With the notation of
Lemma
4.2
, if
n>j-r
and
j\geq r
, then

H\coloneqq x^{u(n-j+r)}y^{v(j-r)}y^{b}
is an element of

(x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J
satisfying

(4.1)

\deg_{y}H=v(j-r)+b\leq jv\quad\text{ and }\quad\deg_{x}H=(n-j+r)u,

cf. Figure
4
. Consequently, for

n>j-r
,

f\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J\right)\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\deg_{x}f\leq(n-j+r)u,

and equality can only hold if
H=f
. In particular, if there exists

f\in\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathsf{G}((x^{u},y^{v})^{n}J)
with

\deg_{x}f=(n+r-j)u
, then
H=f\in\mathcal{U}_{j}
which,
considering the
y
-degree of
H
, further implies
r=\frac{b}{v}

and
\deg_{y}(f)=\dist_{y}(J)
.

We are now set to formalize the pattern repetition mentioned at the
beginning of this section. Based on the
y
-degrees, we define three
subideals
L
,
M
, and
R
of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J
. We use these to
assemble the minimal generators of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J
for all

\ell\geq 0
by suitable shifts.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
u
,
v\in\mathbb{N}
and
J\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be an anchored monomial
ideal. Then, for all

r\geq\left\lceil\frac{\dist_{y}\!J}{v}\right\rceil
and

\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,

(4.2)

\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)=y^{v\ell}L\uplus\biguplus_{j=1}^{\ell}x^{uj}y^{v(\ell-j)}M\uplus x^{u\ell}R,

where

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}f\geq rv\right\}\!,

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,rv\leq\deg_{y}f<(r+1)v\right\}\!,\text{ and}

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}f<rv\right\}\!.

In particular,

\mu\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)=\mu\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)+\ell\cdot|M|.

Proof.

Note that the count is an immediate consequence of the first
assertion.

With the notation of Lemma
4.2
, we have

(4.3)

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J=\mathcal{L}_{r}\uplus\biguplus_{j=r}^{r+\ell-1}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{L}_{j+1})\uplus\mathcal{U}_{r+\ell}.

We claim that the following three statements hold:

(1)

\mathcal{U}_{r+\ell}\cap\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)=y^{v\ell}\cdot L
,

(2)

\mathcal{L}_{r}\cap\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)=x^{u\ell}\cdot R
, and

(3)

for all
r\leq j\leq r+\ell-1
,

(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{L}_{j+1})\cap\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)=x^{u(r+\ell-j)}y^{v(j-r)}\cdot M.

If the claim holds, then the assertion follows
from (
4.3
) since, arranging the sets in reverse
order,

\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)\cap\biguplus_{j=r}^{r+\ell-1}(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{L}_{j+1})=\biguplus_{j=1}^{\ell}x^{uj}y^{v(\ell-j)}\cdot M.

In all three cases, the inequalities that the
y
-degrees must
satisfy are the same on both sides of the equality. Moreover, the
inclusions “
\subseteq
” all hold due to
Lemma
4.2
and the fact that a generator

g\cdot f
of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J
with

g\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}
and
f\in J
can only be minimal,
provided that
f
is a minimal generator of
J
(the same holds for

g
but is not relevant here).

For the reverse inclusions, it is in all three cases left to show
that every element of the set on the right is a minimal generator of

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J
. To do so, take

H=x^{u(r+1+\ell-i)}y^{vi}h\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J

with
h\in J
and
0\leq i\leq\ell+r+1
.

(
1
) Let
F=y^{v\ell}f
with

f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)
and
\deg_{y}f\geq rv
. Then

\deg_{x}F=\deg_{x}f\leq(r+1)u
,
cf. Remark
4.3
. If
H
divides

F
, then
\deg_{x}H\leq\deg_{x}F
implies
(r+1+\ell-i)u\leq(r+1)u
,
that is,
i\geq\ell
. We cancel out
y^{v\ell}
to conclude that

x^{u(r+1+\ell-i)}y^{v(i-\ell)}h\mid f,

which, since
f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)
, implies that
H=F
.

(
2
) Let
F=x^{u\ell}f
with

f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)
and
\deg_{y}f<rv
. Again,
assume that
H
divides
F
. Then
\deg_{y}H=iv+\deg_{y}h<rv
and hence

i<r
. This implies that
r+\ell+1-i>\ell+1
, thus we can cancel
out
x^{u\ell}
on both sides and end up with the same conclusion as
in (
1
).

(
3
) Let
r\leq j\leq r+\ell-1
and

F=x^{u(r+\ell-j)}y^{v(j-r)}f
with
f\in M
. Hence,

\deg_{y}F<v(j-r)+v(r+1)=v(j+1).

As above, if
H\mid F
, then the
y
-degree of

H
must be less or equal than the
y
-degree of
F
, and therefore

i\leq j+1
. This implies
r+\ell+1-i\geq r+\ell-j
, so we can cancel
out
x^{u(r+\ell-j)}
which leaves us with

x^{u(j-i+1)}y^{vi}h\mid y^{v(j-r)}f.

Since
f\in M\subseteq L
, it follows from (
1
) that

y^{v(j-r)}f
is a minimal generator of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{j+1}J
. We
conclude that
H=F
.
∎

Let us unravel Theorem
4.4
in an
example.

Example 4.5
.

Let
u=3
,
v=4
, and

J=(y^{10},x^{2}y^{7},x^{3}y^{5},x^{5}y^{4},x^{7}y^{2},x^{9})

as depicted in the (gray) rectangle in
Figure
5
. We choose
r=3
, which is the
minimal possible choice. The lower two dashed lines in the figure
mark the areas where the
y
-degree is in between
rv
and
(r+1)v
.

The left part of Figure
5
shows

(x^{3},y^{4})^{r+1}J
. The set
L
consists of generators above
the line
j=r
, marked with (blue) circles. The set
R

consists of the (orange) squares below
j=r
. The middle set

M
contains the two encircled (in red) generators between
the two lines
j=r
and
j=r+1
.

On the right side of Figure
5
, we see

(x^{3},y^{4})^{r+3}J
. The minimal generators are a disjoint union of
the sets

y^{4\cdot 2}L,\quad x^{3}y^{4}M,\quad x^{3\cdot 2}M,\quad\text{and}\quad x^{3\cdot 2}R.

J
j=r+1
j=r

(a)

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J

(b)

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+3}J

Fig. 5
.
Visualization of the sets
L
,
M
, and

R
in Example
4.5
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Let
u
,
v\in\mathbb{N}
,
J
be an anchored monomial ideal,

r\geq\left\lceil\frac{\dist_{y}\!J}{v}\right\rceil
, and

M=\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right)\,\,\middle|\,\,rv\leq\deg_{y}f<(r+1)v\right\}\!.

Then
M\neq\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
b\coloneqq\dist_{y}\!J
and choose any natural number

\ell>\frac{b}{v}
. Assume that
M=\emptyset
. Then, by (
3
)
in the proof of Theorem
4.4
, we
have

(\mathcal{U}_{j}\cap\mathcal{L}_{j+1})\cap\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+\ell+1}J\right)=\emptyset

for all
r\leq j\leq r+\ell+1
. Consequently, there are no minimal
generators of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+\ell+1}J
satisfying

(4.4)

rv\leq\deg_{y}f<(r+\ell)v.

However, by the choice of
\ell
, the element

f\coloneqq y^{rv}x^{(\ell+1)u}y^{b}\in(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+\ell+1}J
fulfills
these inequalities. Hence there must be a minimal generator

g\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+\ell+1}J\right)
dividing
f
. Then

\deg_{x}g\leq(\ell+1)u
and by (
4.4
),

\deg_{y}g<rv
must hold, and hence
g\notin(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+\ell+1}J
, a
contradiction.
∎

Theorem
4.4
describes how the minimal
generators of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J
change in a predictable pattern
as
\ell
increases. In other words, the staircase of the ideal

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J
is formed by aligning the staircases of
certain repeatedly occurring subideals: from left to right, we begin
with the staircase of
(L)
, then we repeat the staircase of

(M)
for
\ell
times, and finally add the
staircase of
(R)
.

We want to formalize this idea of “connecting” staircases.

Definition 4.7
.

I
,
J\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]

with
h_{i}
, and we set
h_{0}\coloneqq y^{\dist_{y}I}
and

h_{k+1}\coloneqq x^{\dist_{x}I}
. Then it follows that

I=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\gcd(h_{i},h_{i+1})J_{i}

and, in particular,
I:\gcd(h_{i},h_{i+1})=J_{i}
for all

0\leq i\leq k
.

With the necessary tools in place, we now return to the goal of
expressing
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1+\ell}J
as the link of the ideals generated
by
L
,
M
, and
R
. However, there are
“gaps” between the staircases of these ideals, so we must first
expand them by suitable link points.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
u
,
v\in\mathbb{N}
,
J\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be an anchored monomial

r\geq\left\lceil\frac{\dist_{y}\!J}{v}\right\rceil
. Further, we set
g\coloneqq x^{\alpha}y^{\beta}
to be the

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J
with

\beta=\min\!\left\{\deg_{y}f\,\,\middle|\,\,f\in\mathsf{G}\!\left((x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J\right),\deg_{y}f\geq rv\right\}\!.

L
,

, and

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(M\cup\{x^{\alpha-u}y^{\beta+v}\}\right):x^{\alpha-u}y^{\beta},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(R\cup\{g\}\right):x^{\alpha},

,

Remark 4.14
.

Corollary
4.13
confirms that

r
-segments behave as intended and rephrases
Theorem
4.4
in the language of
ideal links. Continuing Figures
5

and
6
, we provide a visualization in
Figure
7
.

Fig. 7
.
In

(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+3}J
, the staircase of
\mathsf{H}
(striped black) is
repeated
\ell=2
times in the middle. On the top left (in blue)
is the staircase of
\mathsf{A}
and on the bottom right (in green)
is the staircase of
\mathsf{B}
. Note that there is a (shifted)
copy of
\mathsf{H}
included in
\mathsf{A}
.

Remark 4.15
.

With the notation of Definition
4.11
,

\beta<(r+1)v\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\leq(r+1)u

holds. The first inequality follows from
Corollary
4.6
, while the second follows from
Remark
4.3
, since by definition,

g=x^{\alpha}y^{\beta}
is a minimal generator of
(x^{u},y^{v})^{r+1}J

that is in
\mathcal{U}_{r}
. As noted in
Remark
4.3
, if we have equality

\alpha=(r+1)u
, then this implies
rv=\dist_{y}J
,

g=x^{(r+1)u}y^{\dist_{y}(J)}
, and
\beta=\dist_{y}(J)
.

We are now set to prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 4.16
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be an anchored monomial ideal such that

\mathsf{G}(I)=P^{\ast}(I)=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{k+1}\}
and the
g_{i}
are
ordered in descending
y
-degree. Further, let
J\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]

be an anchored monomial ideal, and for
1\leq i\leq k
, let

v_{i}\coloneqq\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})
,

u_{i}\coloneqq\dist_{x}(g_{i},g_{i+1})
, and

r\geq\left\lceil\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\!\left\{\frac{\dist_{y}\!J}{v_{i}}\right\}\right\rceil.

,
\mathsf{A}_{i}
,

,
\mathsf{B}_{i}
are
the
r
-segments of
(g_{i},g_{i+1})J
,

\mathsf{C}_{i}\coloneqq\mathsf{B}_{i}\cdot y^{(r+1)v_{i+1}-\dist_{y}(\mathsf{B}_{i+1})}+\mathsf{A}_{i+1}\cdot x^{(r+1)u_{i}-\dist_{x}(\mathsf{A}_{i})},

\mathsf{C}_{0}\coloneqq\mathsf{A}_{1}
, and
\mathsf{C}_{k}\coloneqq\mathsf{B}_{k}
.

(r+1)v_{i+1}-\dist_{y}(\mathsf{B}_{i+1})
(r+1)u_{i}-\dist_{x}(\mathsf{A}_{i})
g_{i+1}^{r+1+\ell}

Fig. 8
.
Left: The lower section of
(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{r+1+\ell}J

and the upper section of
(g_{i+1},g_{i+2})^{r+1+\ell}J
, where

g_{i+1}^{r+1+\ell}
appears as the lowest and uppermost (red)
dot, respectively. Right:
\mathsf{B}_{i}
(dotted green) and

\mathsf{A}_{i+1}
(shaded blue) overlap at
g_{i+1}^{r+1+\ell}

resulting in a new staircase, namely that of
\mathsf{C}_{i}
. Note
the required shifts in
x
- and
y
-direction to align

\mathsf{B}_{i}
and
\mathsf{A}_{i+1}
before summing them up.

Proof.

Throughout, we use the notation

\alpha_{i}\coloneqq\dist_{x}(\mathsf{A}_{i})
and

\beta_{i}\coloneqq\dist_{y}(\mathsf{B}_{i})
. By
Remark
4.15
,

(4.5)

(r+1)u_{i}-\alpha_{i}\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad(r+1)v_{i+1}-\beta_{i+1}>0.

We argue that for
1\leq i\leq k-1

(4.6)

\dist_{x}(\mathsf{C}_{i})=\alpha_{i+1}+u_{i}(r+1)-\alpha_{i}

and

(4.7)

\dist_{y}(\mathsf{C}_{i})=\beta_{i}+v_{i+1}(r+1)-\beta_{i+1}.

(
4.6
): Both ideals
\mathsf{A}_{i+1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{i}
are
anchored. Note that
\mathsf{A}_{i+1}\cdot x^{(r+1)u_{i}-\alpha_{i}}

contains a minimal generator of
y
-degree equal
0
whereas all
elements in
\mathsf{B}_{i}\cdot y^{(r+1)v_{i+1}-\beta_{i+1}}
have
positive
y
-degree. It follows that

\dist_{x}(\mathsf{C}_{i})=\dist_{x}(\mathsf{A}_{i+1}\cdot x^{(r+1)u_{i}-\alpha_{i}})=\alpha_{i+1}+(r+1)u_{i}-\alpha_{i}.

(
4.7
): If
(r+1)u_{i}-\alpha_{i}>0
, then we can argue
analogously to the above. If
(r+1)u_{i}-\alpha_{i}=0
, then
Remark
4.15
implies that

rv_{i}=\dist_{y}\!J
and

\beta_{i}=\dist_{y}(\mathsf{B}_{i})=\dist_{y}\!J
. Since

\dist_{y}\mathsf{C}_{i}
is given by the maximum of the
y
-degrees of the
two summands of
\mathsf{C}_{i}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\dist_{y}\mathsf{C}_{i}=\max\{\beta_{i}+(r+1)v_{i+1}-\beta_{i+1},\dist_{y}\mathsf{A}_{i+1}\},

and, by Remark
4.12
,

\dist_{y}\mathsf{A}_{i+1}=(r+1)v_{i+1}+\dist_{y}\!J-\beta_{i+1}
, the
assertion follows.

We turn our attention to the assertion of the theorem and proceed by
induction on
k
. The base case
k=1
is
Corollary
4.13
.

Now let
k>1
. By the assumption that
(g_{1},\dots,g_{k+1})
is
anchored, we have
\gcd(g_{1},\dots,g_{k})=y^{\deg_{y}g_{k}}=y^{v_{k}}
.
Using this, we apply the induction hypothesis to the anchored ideal

(g_{1}/y^{v_{k}},\dots,g_{k}/y^{v_{k}})
to conclude that

\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\left((g_{i},g_{i+1})^{r+1+\ell}J\right)=\left(L\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.13657pt}{-1.25pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.34 -1.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}R\right)\cdot x^{u(r+1+\ell)},

which is equal to

(4.9)

\left(Ly^{\beta_{k-1}}+\mathsf{B}_{k-1}x^{\dist_{x}\!L}\right)\cdot y^{v_{k}(r+1+\ell)}+\left(\mathsf{A}_{k}y^{\dist_{y}\!R}+Rx^{\alpha_{k}}\right)\cdot x^{u(r+1+\ell)}.

Before we continue manipulating (
4.9
), we verify a
few handy equations. Note that
\dist_{x}(\mathsf{H}_{i})=u_{i}
and

\dist_{y}(\mathsf{H}_{i})=v_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
, and recall that

u=\deg_{x}g_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}u_{i}
. Thus, using (
4.6
),

\displaystyle\dist_{x}\!L

\displaystyle=\alpha_{1}+\ell\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}u_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}\dist_{x}(\mathsf{C}_{i})=\alpha_{k-1}+\ell u+(r+1)\sum_{i=1}^{k-2}u_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{k-1}+(r+1+\ell)u-(r+1)u_{k-1}.

It follows that

(4.10)

\dist_{x}(L\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 9.53 0 C 9.53 5.26 5.26 9.53 0 9.53 C -5.26 9.53 -9.53 5.26 -9.53 0 C -9.53 -5.26 -5.26 -9.53 0 -9.53 C 5.26 -9.53 9.53 -5.26 9.53 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.13657pt}{-1.25pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -4.34 -1.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}}\mathsf{C}_{k-1})=\dist_{x}\!L+\dist_{x}(\mathsf{C}_{k-1})=\alpha_{k}+(\ell+r+1)u

and

(4.11)

u(r+1+\ell)-\dist_{x}\!L=(r+1)u_{k-1}-\alpha_{k-1}\geq 0,

where the last inequality comes
from (
4.5
). Moreover, since

\dist_{y}\!R=\beta_{k}+\ell v_{k}
we have
(using (
4.5
) again)

(4.12)

v_{k}(r+1+\ell)-\dist_{y}\!R=(r+1)v_{k}-\beta_{k}>0

and, using (
4.7
),

(4.13)

\dist_{y}(\mathsf{C}_{k-1}\mathbin{\raisebox{-0.85pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{\hbox to14.17pt{\vbox to14.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.08563pt\lower-7.08563pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Using (

) and (

), the middle two summands

Corollary 4.17
.

With the assumptions and notation from
Theorem
4.16
, we have

\displaystyle\mu\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{r+\ell+1}J\right)=1+\sum_{i=0}^{k}(\mu(\mathsf{C}_{i})-1)+\ell\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\mu(\mathsf{H}_{i})-1\right)\!.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of
Theorem
4.16
in combination with
Remark
4.9
.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

We argue that it is
not
required to know the
r
-segments
of the individual summands
(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{r+1}J
to compute

\mathsf{C}_{i}
and
\mathsf{H}_{i}
in
Theorem
4.16
.

For
\mathsf{C}_{i}
: The link points
h_{1}
,
\dots
,

of

can be determined directly from

by their

(2)

For
\mathsf{H}_{i}
: By Remark
4.12
and
Theorem
4.4
, we have

\mathsf{H}_{i}=\left((g_{i},g_{i+1})^{r+1}J\right):\frac{h_{i}}{x^{u_{i}}}.

We can immediately conclude that

S:\frac{h_{1}}{x^{u_{1}}}=\left((g_{1},g_{2})^{r+1}J\right):\frac{h_{1}}{x^{u_{1}}}=\mathsf{H}_{1}.

Further, we know by Remark
4.15
, that

\frac{h_{i}}{x^{u_{i}}}\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{H}_{i})
is contained in

T\coloneqq\{g\in\mathsf{k}[x,y]\mid\deg_{y}h_{i}\leq\deg_{y}g\leq\deg_{y}h_{i}+v_{i}\}.

A straight-forward verification shows that for
2\leq i\leq k
,

\gcd(h_{i-1},h_{i})\mathsf{B}_{i-1}\cdot y^{(r+1)v_{i}-\dist_{y}(\mathsf{B}_{i})}\cap T=g_{i}^{r+1}J\cap T.

This further implies that

S\cap T=(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{r+1}J\cap T
and thus,

\mathsf{H}_{i}=S:\frac{h_{i}}{x^{u_{i}}}.

5.
Minimal generating sets of powers

We now apply the preceding results to describe the minimal generators
of large powers of an ideal
I
.

Convention 5.1
.

Throughout, let
I
be an anchored monomial ideal and

P=\{g_{1},\dots,g_{k+1}\}
such that

P(I)\subseteq P\subseteq P^{\ast}(I)
and the
g_{i}
are ordered in
descending
y
-degree. Further, with
\delta_{P}
as in
Notation
3.9
, and
d_{P}
as in
Notation
3.15
, let

D\geq D_{P}\coloneqq(\mu(I)-|P|)\cdot\delta_{P}+|P|\cdot d_{P}
, set

r_{y}(P,D)\coloneqq\left\lceil D\cdot\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\frac{\dist_{y}\!I}{\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})}\right\rceil,

and let
s\geq D+r_{y}(P,D)+1
.

.

We first use Theorem
3.16
to establish that
for all
\ell\geq 0

I^{D+\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(g_{i},g_{i+1})^{\ell}I^{D}

holds. Note that the weakly persistent generators of the ideal
generated by
P
are precisely the elements of
P
. Further, we
observe that
\dist_{y}(I^{D})=D\dist_{y}\!I
and hence

r\coloneqq s-D-1\geq r_{y}(P,D)=\left\lceil\max_{1\leq i\leq k}\frac{\dist_{y}(I^{D})}{\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})}\right\rceil\!.

4.16
with

Definition 5.3
.

We call the sets
\mathsf{H}_{i}
and
\mathsf{C}_{i}
the
(s,y)
-stable

of

with respect to

and

. Note that these
components can be determined directly from

without having to
compute the individual

-segments, see Remark
4.18
with

J=I^{D}
.

The preceding arguments complete the proof of the main theorem of this
section, which we state here.

.

. They are ordered

, and fulfill

\deg_{y}g_{i+1}^{s}\leq\deg_{y}h_{i}^{(y)}\leq\deg_{y}g_{i}^{r+1}.

The lower bound is due to

r\geq r_{y}(P,D)\geq\frac{D\dist_{y}I}{\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})}
and the
upper bound follows from Corollary
4.6
.

Remark 5.6
.

At the beginning of Section
4
we chose
to partition elements of ideals with regular staircase factors based
on their

-degrees (Lemma

,

). Consequently, definitions and

-degree.

Notation 5.7
.

With the notation of
Theorem
5.4
, let

r(P,D)\coloneqq\min\{r_{x}(P,D),r_{y}(P,D)\}.

Remark 5.8
.

Theorem
5.4
states that all
information about large powers of
I
is encoded in
I^{s}
for any

s\geq D+r(P,D)+1
.

Corollary 5.9
.

For
s\geq D_{P}+r(P,D_{P})+1
and
\ell\geq 0
, the computation of

\mathsf{G}(I^{s+\ell})
from
\mathsf{G}(I^{s})
takes

\mathcal{O}(\ell)
additions of (monomial) exponents.

Remark 5.10
.

Theorem
5.4
leads to
significantly faster computations of large powers of
I
. For a
runtime comparison we refer to Section
6
.

Recall that the Hilbert function of the fibre ring of a monomial
ideal—which counts the number of generators of its powers—eventually
becomes a polynomial function
(cf.
[
8
, Theorem 6.1.3]
).
Theorem
5.4
yields an explicit description of this
polynomial.

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be a monomial ideal and

s\geq D+r(P,D)+1
. Then for all
\ell\geq 0
,

\mu(I^{s+\ell})=\mu(I^{s})+\ell\left(\mu(I^{s+1})-\mu(I^{s})\right)\!.

Remark 5.12
.

For
\bullet\in\{x,y\}
and
|P|>2
, we have

r_{\bullet}(P,D_{P})\leq D_{P}\dist_{\bullet}(I)
and

D_{P}\leq\mu(I)(\dist_{\bullet}I-1)
. Thus

\displaystyle D_{P}+r_{\bullet}(P,D_{P})+1\leq\mu(I)\Big(\dist_{\bullet}(I)^{2}-1\Big)+1.

If
|P|=2
, then
r(P,D_{P})=D_{P}
and hence

\displaystyle D_{P}+r(P,D_{P})+1=2(\mu(I)-2)\Big(\min\{\dist_{x}I,\dist_{y}I\}-1\Big)+1.

Corollary 5.13
.

Let
I\subseteq\mathsf{k}[x,y]
be a monomial ideal with
P(I)=\{x^{a},y^{b}\}
,

d\coloneqq\min\{a,b\}
, and

Remark 5.14
.

If
P=\{x^{a},y^{b}\}
, then the
(s-D_{P}-1)
-segments of

(x^{a},y^{b})I^{D_{P}}
(w.r.t.
y

4.11
) coincide with the

(s,y)
-stable components of
I
with respect to
P
, i.e.,

\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{C}_{0},\quad\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{H}_{1},\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathsf{B}=\mathsf{C}_{1}.

We now summarize how the minimal generators of
I^{s}
,
I^{s+1}
, and

I^{s+2}
can be explicitly described from the
(s,\bullet)
-stable
components.

For better readability we switch back to stating the results with
respect to the variable
y
.

we
conclude that
m_{i}=h_{i}g_{i+1}
.

The monomials
w_{1}
,
m_{1}
,
w_{2}
, …,
w_{k}
,
m_{k}
are the
link points of the link (
5.1
) from left to right. With

m_{0}\coloneqq h_{0}g_{1}
,
w_{k+1}\coloneqq h_{k+1}g_{k+1}
and
Remark
4.10
, we have

I^{s+1}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\gcd(m_{i},w_{i+1})\mathsf{C}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gcd(w_{i},m_{i})\mathsf{H}_{i}.

A straightforward verification shows that for
1\leq i\leq k

\gcd(m_{i},w_{i+1})=g_{i+1}q_{i}\quad\text{ and }\quad\gcd(m_{i},w_{i})=\gcd(g_{i},g_{i+1})h_{i}=g_{i+1}\hat{q}_{i}.

Finally, it follows from the definition of the link that the
individual summands intersect exactly at the linking points which
are excluded in
\mathsf{G}^{\ast}\!(\mathsf{H}_{i})
and
\mathsf{G}^{\ast}\!(\mathsf{C}_{i})
,
making them pairwise disjoint. This proves the second assertion.

For the third assertion, we proceed analogously. We determine the

which are
w_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}g_{i}^{2}
,
u_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}g_{i}g_{i+1}
,
and
m_{i}\coloneqq h_{i}g_{i+1}^{2}
. With
m_{0}\coloneqq h_{0}g_{1}^{2}
and

w_{k+1}\coloneqq h_{k+1}g_{k+1}^{2}
, we have

I^{s+2}=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\gcd(m_{i},w_{i+1})\mathsf{C}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gcd(w_{i},u_{i})\mathsf{H}_{i}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gcd(u_{i},m_{i})\mathsf{H}_{i}.

Since
\gcd(m_{i},w_{i+1})=g_{i+1}^{2}q_{i}
,

\gcd(u_{i},m_{i})=g_{i+1}^{2}\widehat{q_{i}}
, and

\gcd(u_{i},w_{i})=g_{i}g_{i+1}\widehat{q_{i}}
, the third assertion
follows.
∎

Remark 5.16
.

It follows from the corollary and its proof that

\mathsf{H}_{i}=I^{s+1}:g_{i+1}\hat{q}_{i}
.

Remark 5.17
.

Corollary
5.15
shows how the minimal
generators of
I^{s+1}
and
I^{s+2}
are computed from

\mathsf{G}(I^{s})
. For
I^{s+\ell}
one multiplies the
q_{i}\mathsf{C}_{i}

with
g_{i+1}^{\ell}
, and
\widehat{q}_{i}\mathsf{H}_{i}
with

g_{i}^{\ell_{1}}g_{i+1}^{\ell_{2}}
for all
\ell_{1}
,
\ell_{2}
with

\ell_{1}+\ell_{2}=\ell
.

Corollary 5.18
.

With the assumptions and notation of
Corollary
5.15
,

\mathsf{G}(I^{s+1})=\biguplus_{f\in\mathsf{G}(I^{s})}f\cdot G_{f},

where, with the notation
v_{i}\coloneqq\dist_{y}(g_{i},g_{i+1})
,

G_{f}=\begin{cases}\{g_{i}\}&\text{if }1\leq i\leq k\text{ with }\deg_{y}h_{i}+v_{i}<\deg_{y}f<\deg_{y}h_{i-1},\\
\{g_{i},g_{i+1}\}&\text{if }1\leq i\leq k\text{ with }\deg_{y}h_{i}\leq\deg_{y}f\leq\deg_{y}h_{i}+v_{i},\\
\{g_{k+1}\}&\text{if }\deg_{y}f<\deg_{y}h_{k}.\end{cases}

Proof.

Observe that for
1\leq i\leq k+1
,

\displaystyle S_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}(I^{s})\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}h_{i}+v_{i}<\deg_{y}f<\deg_{y}h_{i-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}(I^{s})\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}h_{i}\leq\deg_{y}f\leq\deg_{y}h_{i-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{G}(q_{i-1}\mathsf{C}_{i-1}).

Moreover, for
1\leq i\leq k
,

\displaystyle T_{i}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}(I^{s})\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}h_{i}\leq\deg_{y}f\leq\deg_{y}h_{i}+v_{i}\right\}=\mathsf{G}(\hat{q}_{i}\mathsf{H}_{i})\subseteq\mathsf{G}(q_{i-1}\mathsf{C}_{i-1}).

Finally,

T_{k+1}\coloneqq\left\{f\in\mathsf{G}(I^{s})\,\,\middle|\,\,\deg_{y}f<\deg_{y}h_{k}\right\}\subseteq\mathsf{G}(q_{k}\mathsf{C}_{k}).

Note that the sets
S_{i}
and
T_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k+1
form a
partition of
\mathsf{G}(I^{s})
. The assertion now follows from
Corollary
5.15
.
∎

Corollary 5.19
.

With the assumptions and notation of
Corollary
5.15
, let
\ell\in\{1,2\}
,

g\in\mathsf{G}(I^{s+\ell})
and
i
such that

\deg_{y}g_{i+1}^{s+\ell}\leq\deg_{y}g\leq\deg_{y}g_{i}^{s+\ell}
.

If
\ell=1
, then

g\in\begin{cases}g_{i}\mathsf{G}(I^{s})&\text{ if }\deg_{y}g\geq\deg_{y}h_{i}g_{i}\\
g_{i+1}\mathsf{G}(I^{s})&\text{ if }\deg_{y}g\leq\deg_{y}h_{i}g_{i}.\end{cases}

If
\ell=2
, then

g\in\begin{cases}g_{i}\mathsf{G}(I^{s+1})&\text{ if }\deg_{y}g\geq\deg_{y}h_{i}g_{i}g_{i+1}\\
g_{i+1}\mathsf{G}(I^{s+1})&\text{ if }\deg_{y}g\leq\deg_{y}h_{i}g_{i}g_{i+1}.\end{cases}

Proof.

In the proof of Corollary
5.15
, we have
seen that
h_{i}g_{i}
is the link point between

g_{i+1}\Big(q_{i}\mathsf{C}_{i}+\widehat{q}_{i}\mathsf{H}_{i}\Big)
and

g_{i}q_{i}\mathsf{C}_{i}
. Moreover,
\deg_{y}h_{i}g_{i}g_{i+1}
is the link
point between

g_{i+1}^{2}\Big(q_{i}\mathsf{C}_{i}+\widehat{q}_{i}\mathsf{H}_{i}\Big)
and

g_{i}g_{i+1}\widehat{q}_{i}\mathsf{H}_{i}+g_{i}^{2}q_{i}\mathsf{C}_{i}
. The assertion
follows from a comparison of degrees.
∎

We now present examples to conclude this section.

Example 5.20
.

Let
I=(y^{2},x^{2}y,x^{3})
. We apply
Corollary
5.13
with

P=P(I)=\{y^{2},x^{3}\}

to give a complete description of the generators of large powers of

I
.

(1)

We start by computing
D_{P}=1
,

r=r_{x}(P,D_{P})=r_{y}(P,D_{P})=1
, and
s=3
.

(2)

Next, we compute that
h^{y}_{1}=x^{6}y^{2}
is the minimal
generator of
I^{3}
with
y
-degree at least
r\cdot 2+0=2
. Recall
h^{y}_{0}=y^{6}
and
h^{y}_{2}=x^{9}
.

(3)

We compute the
(3,y)
-stable components with respect to
P
:

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{0}

\displaystyle=I^{3}:y^{2}=(y^{4},x^{2}y^{3},x^{3}y^{2},x^{5}y,x^{6}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=I^{3}:x^{3}y^{2}=(y^{2},x^{2}y,x^{3}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}

\displaystyle=I^{3}:x^{6}=(y^{2},x^{2}y,x^{3}).

Recall that
\mathsf{G}^{\ast}\!(J)
denotes the minimal generating set of
a monomial ideal
J
excluding the minimal generator of maximal

y
-degree.

From this we obtain that for all
\ell\geq 0
,

and hence

\mathsf{G}(I^{3+\ell})=y^{2+2\ell}\mathsf{G}(\mathsf{C}_{0})\uplus\biguplus_{j=1}^{\ell}x^{3+3j}y^{2+2(\ell-j)}\mathsf{G}^{\ast}\!(\mathsf{H}_{1})\uplus x^{6+3\ell}\mathsf{G}^{\ast}\!(\mathsf{C}_{1}),

where
\mathsf{G}^{\ast}\!(J)
denotes the set of minimal generators of an
ideal
J
, without the minimal generator of largest
y
-degree. In
particular, we have
\mu(I^{3+\ell})=7+2\ell
.

Example 5.21
.

Let

I=\big(y^{10},xy^{9},x^{2}y^{5},x^{4}y^{4},x^{5}y^{3},x^{6}y^{2},x^{12}y,x^{15}\big)
.
The computations for this example are done in
SageMath
2
2

2

section_6.ipynb
as ancillary
file on the arXiv page
https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21466
of
this paper.
. We compute the persistent generators of
I

P(I)=\{y^{10},x^{2}y^{5},x^{6}y^{2},x^{15}\},

and with
P=P(I)
we obtain
D_{P}=40
,
r(P,D_{P})=r_{y}(D,P)=200
, and

s=241
.

With Theorem
3.16
, we compute

I^{241}=\left((y^{10},x^{2}y^{5})^{201}+(x^{2}y^{5},x^{6}y^{2})^{201}+(x^{6}y^{2},x^{15})^{201}\right)I^{40}.

Now, with the notation of
Definition
5.3
,

\displaystyle h^{y}_{0}=y^{2410},\quad h^{y}_{1}=x^{162}y^{2005},\quad h^{y}_{2}=x^{753}y^{1002},\quad h^{y}_{3}=x^{1815}y^{400},\quad h^{y}_{4}=x^{3615},

and hence the
(241,y)
-stable components of
I
with respect to
P

are

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{0}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:y^{2005},

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:x^{162}y^{1002},

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{2}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:x^{753}y^{400},

\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{3}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:x^{1815}.

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{1}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:x^{160}y^{2005},

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{2}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:x^{749}y^{1002},

\displaystyle\mathsf{H}_{3}

\displaystyle=I^{241}:x^{1806}y^{400},

Analogously to the example above, these ideals can be used to write
down the minimal generators of
I^{241+\ell}
explicitly. Further,
it follows that for all
\ell\geq 0
,

\mu(I^{241+\ell})=1688+7\ell.

6.
Runtime in practice

The results of Section
5
provide a method for
faster computations of high powers of monomial ideals. We implemented
this method in SageMath
[
11
]
(Version 9.5) as follows: Given a monomial
ideal
I
, we use the lower bounds for
s
and
D
as in
Theorem
5.4
. In a
preprocessing step, we compute
I^{s}
using
Theorem
3.16
. For this, we compute
I^{D}
by classical
exponentiation. In the implementation we take advantage of the fact
that all ideals involved are bivariate. After that, for higher powers

I^{s+\ell}
for
\ell\geq 0
, we employ
Theorem
5.4
.

We compare the runtime of our implementation against the built-in
ideal exponentiation of Macaulay2
[
7
]
(Version 1.21; ideals are of type

MonomialIdeal
). The results of our comparison are displayed
in Table
2
. These figures suggest a substantial
speed advantage of our implementation. Additionally, we included
runtimes for Macaulay2 when using Theorem
3.16
to
compute
I^{D+\ell}
from
I^{D}
, which already demonstrates
significant runtime improvements. All computations were done on a
machine equipped with an AMD EPYC 9474F 48-Core Processor @4.10GHz
(192 cores) and 1536GB RAM.

We tested the method on four
different ideals, which are provided in an additional
file
2
and choose
P=P(I)
in all four cases.
The ideal from Example
5.21
is
I_{2}
in the table. In the
following, we write
D\coloneqq D_{P}
and
s\coloneqq D+r(P,D)+1
. We use
(s,y)
-stable components whenever
r(P,D)=r_{y}(P,D)
and

(s,x)
-stable components otherwise.

|P(I)|

\dist\!I
